>
> ​@joeschmoe3665 Skepticism is healthy. Replacing the doctrine we're taught with his analysis isn't critical thinking. Instead, the fact that some of his predictions (that many would have previously thought were wildly inaccurate) should at least inspire us to couple the fact, that we know a lot of leaders lie a lot (politicians are among the least trusted profession today, and lots of government leaks revealing structural issues that often aren't really fixed after their discoveries), with studying history (all history is interpretation of sources, and interpretations are often shaped by victors or at least vested interests), and creative critical thinking. He doesn't have to be an oracle to have value - but I would say his methods would lose credibility if he later consistently miss the biggest actions that actually take place.  
>   
> Let's assume he's anti-western. Then what do these lectures accomplish? Maybe some viewers thinking the East has more value than they previously did, and painting the West as more irresponsible and lazy than they previously. At best, I think that would mean that some worker Westerners watching would be slightly less critical of China and Russia. But it would have to spread to powerful people to have any influence on policy, beside maybe a slight increase in protests.  
> To go to such a great length to study history and game theory, risk making bold predictions, just to slightly influence some protesters, doesn't seem like a great trade-off to me - especially not for a deliberate propaganda strategy.  
> Moreover it is already common for a sizable minority of Westerners today to be critical of the West, and I think the audience for these lectures are mostly such people, so I think it is unlikely to change a lot of perceptions.  
> You might have reason to believe he has a blind spot for China and/or Russia, and is ideologically biased, but I would argue that would be far more harmless than deliberate strategy.  
>   
> Lastly a lot of history he talked about is empirically verifiable. Other things are sloppy/inaccurate figures and quotes, and some of the things are clearly based on speculation and interpretation - and possibly some of the mysticism/intuition he claims Russia has. I would personally prefer if he would be more consistent with differentiating which statements are based on speculation, intuition and empirical sources respectively, but given it's a class primarily targeting high-schoolers and laymen, and that he may be used to using intuition a lot, I feel like he is already making fair effort into framing it in a way that works for empirically-minded individuals.  
>   
> Also, bear in mind that I have not watched much else beside this Geo-Strategy series, but I did see some other videos of him where he mentioned things that approach conspiracy theories, except seemingly more coherent and realistic speculation, but maybe he's even weirder than that, I don't know.

Anyone that keeps saying “oh his claims are speculative” or “his predictions aren’t accurate” that’s the whole point of the class. He’s teaching students how to think and to make speculations. Which is pretty much what game theory encourages. Informed speculation. 

I wish people would shut up and just pay attention. If you have a counter argument against anything he says, share it instead so we can have a conversation about it. That’s what education is. Y’all need to unlearn your indoctrination.

> ​@Dave1984-m5p Here are the factual claims from the transcript that are incorrect, with concise corrections and sources:  
>   
> “Sanctions meant Russia could no longer sell oil on the global market.”  
> Correction: The G7/EU imposed an embargo and a price cap, not a total ban. Russia has continued exporting large volumes of crude—especially to India and China—often via non‑Western shipping and insurance.   
>   
> “The United States froze $300 billion of Russian assets in U.S. banks.”  
> Correction: Roughly $300 billion in Russian state assets were immobilized by the G7/EU/Japan/Canada; most are in Europe, especially at Euroclear (Belgium) (≈€190–210 billion), not in U.S. banks.   
>   
> “Ukraine has lost maybe 500,000 men.”  
> Correction: Ukraine’s President Volodymyr Zelenskyy stated 31,000 Ukrainian soldiers had been killed as of Feb. 25, 2024. Independent Western estimates vary for total casualties (killed + wounded), but there is no credible basis for 500,000 Ukrainian killed.   
>   
> “Hamas’s Oct. 7 attack killed about a thousand people.”  
> Correction: About 1,200 people were killed in Israel on Oct. 7, 2023 (with 251 abducted).   
>   
> “Most people who are dying in this war are children.” (re: Gaza)  
> Correction: Children represent a large share but not a majority of reported fatalities; Reuters reported “nearly a third” of those killed were under 18.   
>   
> “The United States blew up the Nord Stream pipeline.”  
> Correction: No authority has officially attributed responsibility. Sweden closed its probe without charges; Germany’s probe has pursued a lead involving a Ukrainian‑linked group/person, but no final determination has been made.   
>   
> “America is giving Ukraine hundreds of billions of dollars.” (plural “hundreds”)  
> Correction: U.S. appropriations related to the Ukraine response have been about $174 billion through early 2025 (allocations/disbursements are lower). That is not “hundreds” (i.e., ≥$200 billion).   
>   
> “America is providing Israel with all the weapons.”  
> Correction: The U.S. is the largest supplier, but not the only one; Germany is also a major supplier, and Israel has a substantial domestic arms industry. SIPRI estimates ~66–69% of Israel’s arms imports came from the U.S., with most of the rest from Germany.   
>   
> “Weapons for Ukraine were taken from stockpiles in Japan …”  
> Correction: Public reporting shows the U.S. drew on stockpiles in Israel and South Korea for 155 mm shells and other items—not Japan.   
>   
> “Saudi Arabia has joined BRICS.” (stated alongside UAE)  
> Correction: The UAE joined, but Saudi Arabia has not formally joined; Riyadh has said the invitation is still under consideration and, as of May 2025, remained undecided.   
>   
> World War II / Lend‑Lease claims:  
> • “America gave $200 billion to the USSR (for free).” — Correction: U.S. Lend‑Lease to the USSR totaled about $11 billion in 1940s dollars (≈$250 billion in today’s dollars).   
> • “One half of all Soviet tanks and aircraft came from the U.S.” — Correction: The U.S. supplied ~13,000 tanks and ~14,000 aircraft, which amounted to roughly 10% of Soviet tanks and ~12% of aircraft, not half.   
>   
> “Germany is completely dependent on Russian resources (gas).”  
> Correction: Germany reduced dependence to near‑zero after 2022, replacing Russian pipeline gas with LNG and other sources.   
>   
> “The U.S. said Russia could not sell its oil at all.” (rephrasing of the first point about a “ban”)  
> Correction: The price‑cap regime allows sales below the cap using Western services; Russia also sells using non‑Western services. It was not a total sales ban.   
>   
> “One quarter of young Americans now believe Osama bin Laden was a good guy.”  
> Correction: There is no reputable polling showing 25%. The most‑cited (and contested) poll put favorable views at ~20% among 18–29‑year‑olds; major pollsters have not validated the “one quarter” claim.   
>   
> If you want, I can also flag statements that are unverifiable/speculative (e.g., that Putin “knew of or encouraged” the Oct. 7 attack) or that are opinions rather than facts.

### **@fedorpakhomov1054** | 👍 122 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=B_al2wgk49Y&lc=UgzWhjNvIz7Dup7Nfgd4AaABAg)

I listened to perhaps 15 minutes in total, but what I am hearing is quite off:
1. Putin does not fight with US because of consumerism. On economics Putin is no less neo-liberal than most of Western politician. Russia is a capitalist country with market economy and very much present consumerism. And this haven't changed because of confrontation with the West.
I personally would argue that the reason of confrontation isn't really ideological, but simply boils down to the inability on the part of US (and to a lesser extent on the part of big EU countries) to make accommodations of Russia for her integration into the Western system on sufficiently respectful conditions.
2. Russia does not continue the war in Ukraine because of the desire to drain NATO. This sounds much more like the Chinese interest here probably would be. Fairly obviously, if an easy and fast victory would have been possible it would have been far more desirable for Russia (and there are good indications that this indeed what the original plan was). The war is costly for Russia and at least in the beginning there was an attempt on Russian part to exit the war on relatively favorable terms (Istanbul negotiations), which was denied.
As for if the goal of achieving full or partial victory in Ukraine, is more about Ukraine herself, or if it is to the large extent it is about the impact the defeat would have on US and EU, it is quite a bit hard to tell.
3. As for presenting Russian philosophy as 'mystical', 'broad', and 'intuitive'. It is largely a sort of a myth. Perhaps it might have had some sort of relevance in 19-th century. But modern day Russian way of thinking is most certainly not mystical and is quite rational (no less so than in UK). As for the other elements, I would tend to agree on broadness (Russians) vs narrowness (British) of thinking. With regards to the opposition logic vs intuition, logic certainly is not alien to Russians (though, maybe there is something to this assessment). 
-----------------------

Though the point of Russia having the strategy of making fractures in the West alliance structure, certainly is true. I listened to a lecture of Andrey Sushentsov (whom is an influential International Relations researcher in Russia) on a broad Russian international relations strategy. And his point was that the broadest long-term goal that Russia is pursuing on decades scale time-frame is the increasing of the sovereignty (i.e. the capacity to make independent self-interested decisions) of various countries in the world that otherwise would have been acting in accordance with US. Thus, of course, damaging US hegemony.

I don't think either that one can boil the whole motivation to go to war to just one reason. Certainly, there is a big part of this that boils down to lack of integration to the west and NATO pressure on Russia. On the other hand, is true that consumerism have shattered russian society, you have to take into account the impact of USSR dissolution and the neo liberal flood that took Russia into the 90's on the russian people. And indeed, war is an unifing factor, this is no mystery.  
> 2- Every war is costly, but in hindsight it seems that Russia got far more favored economically than EU. May be is not as much about draining NATO (which measn draingin US firstly and foremost) but about draining EU, which is indeed happening. The objective here would be to drag EU into further dependance of Russian energy. We can clearly see that EU economy is recieving the greatest hit bc of this war, and after this ends, this would clearly set Russia as the main hegemon of Europe in the multipolar order that is arising. As a matter of fact, we can see that tensions between US and european countries are at its highest point in the history of modern world order.  
> 3- I didn't take his philosphy comparison as a binary choice. Neither Russian philosophy lacks logic, nor british philosophy lacks intuition. It's just about what is the deciding charasteristics and what set them apart. Mysticism was present in Russian thinking at least through the early 20th century, there are even some voices in Russia (e.g Dugin) that claims that one of the mistakes made by soviet leadership was the intent to erase this from their people, in this respect and if this is true, then it would make sense that Putin would try to give religion a bigger place in Russia than it used to have in the last decades.

### **@TlsMS93** | 👍 5 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=B_al2wgk49Y&lc=UgynZk4v-JR7gBpyLxd4AaABAg)

This interpretation of Operation Barbarossa is completely wrong.

Do you really think that Stalin calculated that it would be necessary to be attacked and come close to defeat in Moscow in order to gain the world's sympathy and thus industrialize the country and become a superpower? As if the USSR depended on this. It had amphibious tanks when the rest of the world had none, as if Soviet artillery was not the best in the world, as if Soviet tanks and not German ones served as a model for modern tanks. It depended on Lend-Lease due to Germany's surprise attack and not that Stalin was counting on this, which brings us to the next point.

If Stalin had attacked first as planned, no one would have been against him. The world would have seen him as the “liberator” of Europe and not the conqueror. That is why he accepted an official position in the government in May 1941. He expected to personally “liberate” Europe. Hitler guessed these plans in the summer of 1940 and attacked first. Want more proof that no one would help Hitler if Stalin attacked first? Churchill wrote to Stalin asking for help, how could Stalin help? By attacking Germany. But Stalin trusted Soviet intelligence, which concluded that Germany was not prepared for a war with the USSR and, due to the urgency of the Soviet danger, had no time to prepare.

So, yes, Stalin was a genius, but he made one mistake. In the summer of 1940, he spied on German supply lines in Romania, called Ploiesti, Northern Bukovina, from where he could launch a quick and devastating attack on the German war machine trapped in the west. It was during this same period that Hitler began to instigate the military bureaucracy to plan an offensive against the USSR.

too oversimplified view of 39-41's events.
1) Author totally missed the winter war showase, which did some major shifts in politicians conclusions. Red Army let's say showed not the best results in the war with minor and concidered weak Finland. This alone showed that maybe big and strong red tiger had a paper legs 
2) Molotov-Ribbentrop pact was signed on 23rd of august 1939. Fall Weiss began on 01 september(!!). It's just impossible to play any significant role in decisionmaking within the such narrow timeframe. All military preparations for the invasion had been long done, some diversions already had took place - war machine was on its track and nothing was to stop Poland's invasion.
3) USSR signed non-aggression treaties with France, Britain, Poland, Estonia, Latvia, Lithuania, Turkey and some other countries around the same time - simply beacuse USSR was recognised as a soverign and legitimate -successor of Russian Empire. It was just a common practice in diplomacy to start from that non-aggression treaty. And then move on to other more specific deals.
4) All the terrible points about Stalin's disbeliefs in intels and other crap - just ignore them. It was 1960-s politically motivated myths and legends-spreading. Today's knowlegde is much more complex and deep. Just few examples : 
=in 1941 ussr agents in 3rd reich and Japan sent a reports about Germany's preparations to attack USSR at least 5 times. In march, april, may(twice) and june. Do we want to believe all of them ? or one(which one, btw?) ? it's a harsh reality. 
5) some speculations about readiness of USSR to attack in 1941 are just the same crap. Whith it's ecenomy rebuilding and military reorganising in 1941 it's just not a possibility. Around 25% of industrial capacity of USSR was reconstructing and moving away from western parts, it's airfields were reconstructing with concrete surfaces, some new schedules for army units were approved and began to implement. If we realistically concider this as an option, attack was not possible until 1943 at least(remember, 1940's winter war lessons) - the date when more-or-less Red Army had finished most of it's units changes - technically, structurally and logistically. Guess who knew that too? Germans did. And they felt like strike first in 1941 is a good chance to win and not to gamble with fully supplied and trained Red Army in the future. 
=Next thing about Germany's preparations and massive force concentrations at the border. Yes, it's obvious, en masse of infantry at the borders and what? What infantry can do? Contrary, mobile tank-groups were highly concealed and moved to their attack positions in the mid-night of 21-th june with total secrecy, concealment and silence. So even if some talented scout notices some movement or motor sounds in the night, it just wont be confirmed by air or radio-intel later in the day. 
=No one (aside high command, chief staff etc ofc.) really knew what's coming - even appeal to the troops was read just hours before the attack(!!). Therefore, no deserted soilder could really help USSR with knowledge, even with "secret" documents - because there were no obtainable secret documents until very late (we don't want to believe an enemy soilder with his words only, right?). It is a normal way to provide secrecy to operaion. Just don't think of our precessors like stupid people. They were not. All the memory books like "we felt it in the air" or "we knew what's coming" its just a crap. Don't believe it. Letters, documents and other syncronous to that time sources of information just don't confirm that - exact the opposite - even Japan leaders didn't knew what was to happen. 
Last thing i must notice. Policymaking is an art of possibilities. Just keep it in mind while wathcing it. Until things happen, every possibility must be concidered as possible. There is no defined way in history.

### **@ek7327** | 👍 3 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=B_al2wgk49Y&lc=Ugw4n3uireVTmh2X54x4AaABAg)

The USA gave NOTHING for free to the Soviet Union! Like, they were not dumb 😅

Right after the Civil war, the Soviets started to desperately seek technologies because the Russian Empire was 30-50 years behind the West (actually one of the main underlying causes of the Bolshevik Revolution). They paid American and European engineers to come teach them technology and build plants, bought equipment from abroad, and, refused to be sold technology, they spied and stole it. 

First the Soviet leadership wanted to make the revolution worldwide. The Western elites predictably turned against them, then Hitler wrote a book setting his aim to conquer the Soviet territories. He was quietly supported by the European and USA elites because they hoped that the two aggressors will eliminate each other. The communist plan to spread the revolution using the USSR as a base was put aside due to the imminent threat of the Nazi invasion: those needed only the lands, not the übermensch, and didn’t plan on leaving many Soviet people alive.

When it became clear in 1937 that Hitler was finally set to conquer the USSR and go at war with Europe, Stalin negotiated with France and the Great Britain to form a military alliance. He also tried to persuade the Poles to give the Soviet army a passage through the Polish border should Hitler attack, as first he had to go through Poland to invade Ukraine. The Poles refused. Each European country was hoping it won’t be them who Hitler attacks first. And only after all that diplomacy failed, the Soviets made the infamous Molotov-Ribbentrop pact with Hitler. Of all the parties he was the only one wanting to make pacts. He needed the Soviets to be neutral while conquering Europe.

Meanwhile the USSR had no illusions, they knew they were final Hitler’s aim; they were just bidding for some extra time to produce weapons and move the key plants behind the Urals, out of reach for the enemy’s aviation.

Stalin was aware that the USSR was not ready for war neither in 1937, nor in 1939 or 1941. He also didn’t think that the proud and technologically advanced Europe, so unwilling to ally with the damned communists, would fall so quickly.

It was not Stalin’s plan to lure the Nazi army inside the USSR, it was his greatest miscalculation that Hitler wasn’t ready to attack in 1941, for which the Soviet people paid dearly. Only after the Soviet army turned the tide of war under Stalingrad in 1943 and Europe suffered enough under Hitler’s boot, did the Soviets get the lend-lease from the USA and the second European front was opened. Until that, everyone waited for the nazis and the Soviets to bleed each other dry. 

The US help was very strategic: they gave the Soviets just so much to help crush the Nazis together with other nations, and the Soviets had to pay it back for almost 60 years as did many European countries, which made the USA the richest country in the world after WWII was over.

I’m genuinely astonished at the notion that the USA gave resources and technologies “for free” to the USSR, their greatest ideological rival, thus sponsoring its technological advance and rise to power. Like, were they idiots? The Cold War started in 1945 right after the hot war was over, nuclear bombs were being developed specifically to thwart the communist threat after Hitler is beaten, so what are we even talking about? 

The war was won, and the road to further success of the country was laid by the Soviet and Russian people - not Stalin, not the USA. It’s a popular mistake to overestimate the role of personalities in the Russian history, which is surely huge, but in the end it’s the people who are the main actor behind everything. The West loves Tolstoy, but generally fails to grasp this from his writings. A crazy tsar commanding gutless and obedient slaves, as Russian history is often depicted in the West, can win no wars.

Endurance, goodwill and selflessness, not intuition or mysticality, are the traits of the Russian people that make them suffer, thrive and achieve great things at great costs.

### **@entwine** | 👍 11 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=B_al2wgk49Y&lc=UgyvHlQJdWnN8XnmNIh4AaABAg)

Interesting lecture, but I think you're giving Stalin and Putin the level of strategic and forecasting ability that is simply hard to imagine in a real world with all its complexity.

The analysis where Stalin planned for Hitler to attack USSR and almost get to Moscow (including the siege of Leningrad for 3 years and many millions of people dying) in order to get aid from other countries, form anti-Hitler coalition and conquer Eastern Europe is interesting, but I think it's too much of a galaxy brain take on Stalin. This kind of foresight in a very complex war environment is just hard to imagine and the standard story looks more likely. Also events like the Russo-Finnish war, preceding Hitler's attack don't make much sense in your story: why secure the border and make Leningrad harder to reach if you're planning to give it up just a year later?

Likewise with Putin and Ukraine, the hypothesis that Putin attacked because he aimed to topple Ukrainian government in a few days and continued the war when that failed to avoid looking weak is also much simpler and more compatible with the evidence.

### **@thadjacobs**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=B_al2wgk49Y&lc=UgyD1hXa7SdKKwFejmF4AaABAg)

Greek philosophy is the bedrock of Western intellectual traditions, not only establishing foundational principles of logic, ethics, and governance, but also profoundly shaping generations of leaders and thinkers. Its influence is especially evident among the American Founding Fathers, who studied Greek models of democracy, citizenship, and the rule of law as they crafted a new form of government rooted in ancient ideals yet adapted to modern needs. Enlightenment thinkers likewise drew deeply on Greek rational inquiry and philosophical methods in their formulation of individual rights, the separation of powers, and rational governance. Educators and reformers have consistently built upon Greek concepts such as the Socratic method and the pursuit of virtue, ensuring that Greek philosophical legacies continue to be reinterpreted and integrated into evolving educational and societal frameworks across each historical era.

The West will deal their hardest blow for you to cease,
Russia will deal you damage and each turn will give you opportunity to surrender.

> Famine unfortunately was quite common in the Russian Empire -- one must remember the country that the communists inherited in 1917 was a pre-industrial, mostly illiterate feudal peasantry.  Famine was so common, in fact, that the Russian Empire promised food to anyone who would attend Nicholas II's coronation.  When the food was slow in coming the despiration ran so high that there was a stampede that killed more than 1000 people.  Many of the corpses were hidden under the stage during the coronation -- this event was called the "Khodynka Tragedy."  
>   
> We must also remember that immediately following Stalin's death was an Anti-Stalin USSR leadership lead by ethnic Ukrainians. >

### **@raul3450** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=B_al2wgk49Y&lc=UgzRhKm0snF_X-w06Kd4AaABAg)

The real global influence of the United States lies not primarily in its military might 💣, but rather in its currency’s role as the dominant medium of global trade and reserve store. 

The establishment of the Bretton Woods Agreement in 1944 marked the point at which the U.S. dollar became the anchor of international monetary exchange, and although the U.S. nominal GDP in 1944 was about US $224 billion (in then current dollars)   — far smaller than its economy today — the dollar’s reserve status granted the U.S. outsized structural power. 

As long as other nations accept and use the U.S. dollar in trade and reserves, the United States continues to wield global influence. Once that monetary foundation erodes, the façade of military strength becomes exposed. 

Simply put: military power may grab headlines, but the true levers of geopolitical dominance operate through finance and currency.

### **@schoey102102**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=B_al2wgk49Y&lc=UgyPOOStv-LRY_ZdUmV4AaABAg)

There is some additional context that I thought would have been great in this lecture regarding the Russian psyche in the era of Stalin.

For centuries before Stalin, Russia had been ruled by a Tsar, which is closer to a god than a king. Stalin being the “great man” or “strong man” arguably could only be so if he was tapping into a wealth of servility and reverence for leadership within the Russian people. The people were used to a quasi-god for a leader, so treated new leaders as such. 

I am British so am obviously slightly biased but I think it’s the key difference between us and Russia: it is unthinkable that someone like Stalin, or Hitler, or Putin, could ever arise here in the first place. The national sense of irony and distrust of strong men is arguably what binds us as a nation. You might say it’s why (as was covered in this lecture) Churchill repeatedly told Hitler to do one - Churchill knew the man could not be tolerated or trusted.

### **@thomaswalther7276**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=B_al2wgk49Y&lc=UgytBBuKqFOOZcQKbrR4AaABAg)

Well, a few points here:
2 years BEFORE the War in Ukraine started, a German TV channel had an article about the North Crimea Canal.
This canal starts within Ukraine territory, and after Russia seized the cimea peninsula, the entrance to this canal was closed by Ukraine as retribution.
Now: There is a lot of acriculture on crimea, and most of it depends on water from this canal.
The article concluded: To maintain the agriculture there, Putin would have 2 options: 1) To build massive seawater desalination plants.
OR: 2) To seize the area of the entrance of the canal on Ukrainian soil by force. 
Then, to be able to hold this area, a strip of land would have to be secured connecting the area around Nowa Kachowka, where the canals starts, to the Russian mainland.
The Russian attack in the south of Ukraine did exactly that. Predicted 2 years before the war started.

Another point is: Unrequited love.
After the collapse of the Soviet Union, Russia, a super power for more than half a century, still wanted a seat on the table of the big players. To still be recognized as equals.
Instead, Russia was just used for cheap gas, other than that it was left alone and experienced the worst decade for a long time.
It got the boot on its neck or at least a cold shoulder instead of a hand helping it up. Russia in general and Putin in specific did not forget this.

Buffer Zones.
In cold war, each of the blocks had their "buffer countries" which they were willing to throw at the enemy and keep the actual "core enemy" at a distance.
Remember how allergic the US reacted when Russia tried to place nuclear missiles in Cuba. In 1962 the world was at the brink of a nuclear war, because someone violated someones "buffer zone" and encroached into what they saw as their "backyard".
Now, former "buffer countries" for Russia have joined the opposinf side. From the ukrainian border it is a mere 450 km to reach Moscow.

So, to restore former imperial glory, Putin started a "special operation". 3 forces, from north, east and south to accomplish certain targets:
Restore freshwater supply to crimea, seize a buffer zone around the canal entrance and create a strip to the russian mainland: 100% Achieved.
Seize control of the 4 partially russia-leaning eastern states and the industrial and natural ressources there. Have some rigged elections and make this russian territory: 75% achieved.
But the "special operation" required to complete a 3rd objective to finish this "operation" in a short time: Seize Kiew, capture Selenskiy, install a russia-friendly puppet regime which would recognize the land seized by Russia as Russian territory and come to peace with Russia. Objective #3 failed horribly. The - to what I recall - 70km long trek of military vehicles failed to reach Kiew. Kiew airport, vital for bringing in more troops was captured, but then reclaimed by Ukrainian forces. Capturing Selenskyi was attempted several times, but always failed.
Without a compliant puppet regime in Kiew there is no one who would just give territory to Russia willingly.

So I disagree with Prof Jiang here. This was not planned as a war lasting for years, but as a coup, that in its main part, failed.

I am old enough that I could here the wwII stories of the generation of my grandfather. The Soviet Union did not win against Nazi Germany by superior technology, but through superior numbers. This means both material, like tanks, at that time. But also the willingness to send as many troops to their deaths as it takes in order to win.
Same again in Ukraine. Russia is still advancing, although very slowly. But for each small advance they pay with an enormous amount of young men. Given Russias demography and that it is not really an immigration-friendly country seem to indicate a rather dire future for Russia.
Also: If Russia advances at the same speed it does now, it would take more than 30 years to conquer the whole Ukraine.
Should ukrainian forces one day collapse running out of men to fight, Russia might control whole Ukraine. A vast country full of weapons and full of people who hate Russia. This smells like another Afghanistan to me.

Major mistake here regarding Stalin @~40:00:
Germans could overrun Russia's defenses initially NOT because Stalin gave bad orders. Quite the contrary. It has been established beyond reasonable doubt that the SU was in the finishing stages of mounting an all-out attack on Europe. The Wehrmacht basically overran a vast staging ground area, encountering the biggest invasion forces of all times with its pants down. not only does this explain the astonishing killrates the best, there are dozens (!) of strong pieces of evidence weighing heavily in favour of this theory.
Secondly, Stalin had good reason to mistrusts intelligence as everybody was out to get him. As uncovered relatively recently, the Allies did in fact plan to attack the SU before Barbarossa and these plans were leaked to Stalin (so called "Operation Pike"). Hitler unknowingly thwarted these plans by mere weeks. Otherwise his army might have become the centerpiece of the Allies in a very different war.

### **@jacobmartin1100**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=B_al2wgk49Y&lc=UgzU_BOibuHgthi2Xg54AaABAg)

I wouldn't call "burning your already weak demography and creating massive economic pressure on your own state to the point that it makes itself vulnerable to price blackmailing from China and India" a strategic win for Putin. At this point he's fighting to preserve his own regime as opposed to broader strategic objectives. He was humiliated in Armenia, he essentially lost his one base in the Med when Syria fell, his grip in Africa has both simultaneously increased and become less steady through Wagner being hollowed out, Khazakstan in specific and Central Asia more broadly has more or less officially left the Russian orbit (which it had been surely placed literally just before the war when the FSB helped cement the regime) to the Chinese orbit, basically did nothing while the Iranians were obliterated from the air, lost Maduro, had several tankers (both reflagged and flagged) seized at sea and in port, lost a significant portion of their navy and airforce (including irreplaceable strategic bombers) and the Europeans now buy much more of their gas and oil from the US and the Azeris and are committed, at least in part, to a massive military buildup which they were content to coast off before. There is an argument to be made that it's accelerating American fragmentation, but I think that events in Gaza would have done that on their own, and if the cost of that is essentially "your economy is going to explode if it ever leaves war-footing after your best and brightest fled", then that was a stupid price to pay. The Europeans alone can indefinitely fund a war in Ukraine if they so choose, and if the war endures for long enough for them to fully mobilize their economic base, they could very well end it through weight of material. About the only positive things I can say for Putin is that it allowed him to liquidate his internal opposition from the right and the left, and that the Russians now have the largest drone production capacity on the planet. For an economy the size of Italy's which was already deep in demographic decline, there are not gains that can tip anywhere near the balance, even if they get concessions that give them the territory they already hold (don't even suggest they'd manage to cross the Dinipir river, that's as fanciful as them using nuclear weapons) because the Europeans are going to see that as a threat to their security just as much as the Greenland moves. When push comes to shove, China will throw Russia under the bus if it pulls the Europeans out of the American orbit and prevents them from dispatching aid to the Pacific in the event of war, along with further mutual investment and tech transfers.

### **@kd8437**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=B_al2wgk49Y&lc=Ugz0aPGUZhuogLxCqbZ4AaABAg)

The claim that the United States “industrialized” the Soviet Union through Lend-Lease is a major exaggeration. 
Lend-Lease did not create Soviet industry from scratch, but it did provide the USSR with critically important supplies, vehicles, raw materials, industrial equipment, and communications gear that strengthened the Soviet war effort and helped relieve major bottlenecks.

Radio technology and radio communications already existed in the Soviet Union long before Lend-Lease. It is therefore inaccurate to suggest that the USSR had to acquire radio technology from the United States in order to develop this field at all.

At the same time, the USSR did not merely use American radio and communications equipment supplied through Lend-Lease. These deliveries also gave Soviet specialists access to foreign samples, components, and engineering solutions that could be studied, adapted, and in some cases reproduced domestically.

However, it would be a clear exaggeration to argue that the Soviet radio industry simply grew out of American technology.

### **@homeworld22**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=B_al2wgk49Y&lc=UgzyW2nvwXMeUaDk71d4AaABAg)

Don't quite agree with this idea that it was Stalin who played Hitler and not the other way round.

I get what he's saying about the Soviet's poor strategic position turning around completely from 1939 to 1945, but this seems to be luck more than any particular strategic genius.

I am 100% sure Stalin did not intend to sacrifice 27 million of his own citizens. He did not intend for the Germans to get within 30km of the Kremlin. He may well have been gearing up for an eventual war against Germany but did not expect if would arrive so soon (especially not while Germany was still fighting Britain to its rear).

If he really had adopted a deliberate 'wait for them to come to us' stategy then the Red Army would not have been so decimated in the summer of 1941. They would have fought a well managed delaying action, trading space for time, manning prepared defensive lines one by one and avoiding large scale encirclements until the winter. 

Instead we saw disaster after disaster, until the Red army was so depleted in manpower and materials they were almost overwhelmed, and most certainly would have been if not for the weather and German overconfidence.

### **@Ταυρικήσιδηρίτις**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=B_al2wgk49Y&lc=UgwGbdLc3WAG4Mc5Lm54AaABAg)

You in the West know well about the Molotov-Ribbentrop Pact, but you never mention that many Western powers concluded such pacts with Germany. Tell your flock about the pact between Germany and Poland about the division of Czechoslovakia, for example. The Soviet Union did not consider this an attack on Poland, since it only returned its original territories from history, which Poland had captured before. In another video you talk about Königsberg, but you don't talk about how Prussia fought with the Russian Empire. From January 1758 to July 1762, East Prussia and the city of Königsberg became part of the Russian Empire. The same Kant was a subject of the Russian Empire for some time. Representatives of all classes, including the teaching staff of the local university, swore allegiance to the Russian Empress Elizabeth Petrovna. Immanuel Kant himself took the oath of allegiance. By that time, the once brilliant Duchy of Prussia with its capital in Königsberg had become a backwater after the creation of a united Prussia with its capital in Berlin. But when Russian troops arrived there, the city literally came to life and once again acquired the gloss of a capital with balls and other amusements. It is not surprising that representatives of ancient German families willingly entered the service of the Russian Empire. Thus, the brother of Count von Keyserling, whose children Kant taught, was the envoy of the Russian Empire to the European courts and even the president of the St. Petersburg Academy of Sciences. When Peter III, a great admirer of the Prussian king, ascended the Russian throne after the death of Elizabeth Petrovna, he quickly concluded a peace treaty with Prussia, returning all the territories occupied by Russian troops. Thus, Königsberg returned to East Prussia. But Kant's connection with Russia did not end there. In 1797, he wrote a letter to the St. Petersburg Academy of Sciences asking to correct the error and include him in the list of honorary members of the academy. The fact is that three years before this, his name was recommended to be included in this list, and Kant gave his consent to this, which, apparently, got lost somewhere. His son also became a diplomat at the Russian imperial court. You have an extremely superficial knowledge of history, and you still dare to give some assessments based on poor knowledge.

### **@bbravoo** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=B_al2wgk49Y&lc=UgwdKsvtp65JnmjpiuN4AaABAg)

I do not really buy that Stalin wanted Hitler to invade. 
Sure. Hitler and Stalin both knew that they were sooner or later going to war between themselves. Specially because Hitler had written very clear their hate for communist. 
Stalin was more a paranoid than a genius. He killed a lot of their own generals and spies. That made really difficult for hitler to have good intelligence from Russia and thought that all was going to be finished in three months. 
Stalin truly wanted to appear like a soft, trusty ally and collaborated with Hitler. But this wasn't part of a plan to be attacked, it was a plan to gain time, arm themselves and be the winner between weakened powers after the war.

Also, had Russia attacked Germany, they would probably had been destroyed fast. Attacking is harder than defending. Changing the logistics in Barbarrosa would mean that Russian troops get destroyed in Poland flats. And the Allies, that at this point were without US, would have welcomed this help. They were fighting for survival. In fact, Russia took Berlin and they kept sharing information. 

When Hitler attacks the Russian act in real chaos and get millions captured. Stalin even disappear for some time as if overburdened by the situation. Is not the reaction of "Everything goes according to the plan". 

Rather than a genius Stalin it was an strategical blunder from Hitler. Attack when you have too many open fronts. Hitler was confident after too many victories and conquering half Europe. If Japan had just attacked Singapur leaving the Americans alone maybe Russia wouldn't have received so much help. There wouldn't have been an operation Torch to land on Africa just when Stalingrad starts. 

Sometimes leaders just have good or bad luck. Stalin was cautious and prioritized survival over anything is his country including his people and this bet resulted. But the dice could have landed on the other side. 

That is my opinion. I am not a professional historian and I may be wrong.

Проценты растут, идет инфляция доллара, а вместе с этим капиталы богатейших людей тоже обесцениваются.

1. Genocide (gen*o*cide) -- From the Latin "genos" -- the destruction of a genomic group -- a group with a common and specific ethnicity or genomic makeup.  Or people of a specific nation.

There is no such thing as a Palestinian genome or ethnicity -- as proved by DNA analysis. Most Palestinians are Semitic. They are the same genome as the Israelis. Arabs are also Semitic and the same genome as Israelis. As such, it is not possible for a group to commit genocide against itself. 

There is likewise no Palestinian state -- hence it is not possible to commit genocide against people that do not belong to a nation.

If Palestinian Authority accepted the 2 state solution proposed in 1947 -- then this statement could have been correct. But. They didn't.

2. Putin does not care about America and does not want to destroy it. That is absolute nonsense.

In 1991, when Russia, Ukraine and Belarus decided to dissolve the Soviet Union (it didn't collapse) -- the Russians still owed a Lend / Lease debt to the US.  This debt was only repaid fully in 2006!

As a principal stake holder in Russian debt, the US played a pivotal role in debt restructuring and rewriting of the Russian Constitution -- to be more inline with Western structures of Free Markets and Open Trade..Russian currency was taken off the gold standard (the Bretton Woods System) and pinned to the US Dollar thru the use of the Central Bank and IMF -- affectivley dollarizing the Russian economy and making the Dollar their reserve currency.

 Russian Ruble value is determined by a Currency Committee, rather then the laws of Supply and demand as is the case in Western nations.

By keeping Dollar reserves of Russia low, America is effectively able to control the value of the Ruble -- and force all the Russian Oligarchs to depend on the dollar as reserve currency. This is how US has controlled Russia from 1991 onwards -- and why they were able to put financial sanctions on Russia by freezing the dollar accounts of the Oligarchs. For the past 35 years US has been trying to retain Russia as a puppet regime of the US. So.. were not the good guys in this story.

Dollarization of the Ruble effectively took most power away from the Russian president and made him a puppet of the IMF and the US Federal Reserve. It is described in Section 2 of the Russian constitution.. and this was done under Reagan and later Clinton as part of a deal with the foolish Gorbachev and Yeltsin, to secure new debt that Russian needed for reconstruction.  In simple terms, the Russians got played. So much for Putin being a "dictator"..

The plan of the US -- as was the plan of Brittain, was to fracture Russia into multiple states, force them to pay for their debt by nationalizing and auctioning off land on international market. Then simply buy up the parceled lands -- thereby spending Russia out of existence..in a form of Economics Genocide.  

This plan was originally conceived by Germany and Brittain in 18th and 19th Century -- known as the Great Game. It was the systematic colonization of Russia -- and its the reason why Lithuania, Poland, Estonia, Romania and Bulgaria exist. All of them are the results of Western attempts to Colonize Russia over the past 400 years. 

That was British goals in War of Crimea, Turkish goal in War of 1853, Napoleons goal in 1812 and Hitlers goal in 1939.

The German war machine was funded by US, Brittain, France, Italy and Austria. Staging for Russian Invasion by Germany was in Poland.
Americas proxy war against Russia in Ukraine is just the latest attempt of the west to Colonize Russia.

The plan would have likely worked -- but Yeltsin passed the reigns to Putin. And over a period of the last 25 years, Putin dedillarized most of Russian economy, moving back towards the Gold Standard -- breaking the US dependency of the Oligarchs. Russia bought back much of the land that was parceled off to Western interests -- and is well on track to being a Sovereign State --- so pardon Putin for not wanting to be Washington's bitch -- like Zelensky is. Putin wants to free Russia from the choke hold of the West -- and US is simply mad that this divorce is now inevitible.. because that ends the unipolar US-centric era of WWII -- so we must cooperate with Russia and recognize them as a peer if we want to survive. Russian is central to Bricks nations and they present 76% of the world's population and consumer markets.. The West only presents 17% !!

Ukraine, unfortunately was not able to accomplish this -- and now, nearly 60% of its usable lands are owned by US and other Western interests, by companies like Blackstone Group etc
And thats what we are doing in Ukraine actually -- protecting Ukranian lands owned by American companies, that are hoping to make massive profits exploiting Ukranian resources.  

The richest resources and infrastructure in Ukranian territories were populated and developed by Russians -- in Donbas and Crimea. 2 areas that petitioned UN for independence from Ukraine. They dont want to be part of that broken regime and don't want to be exploited by the West. So thats what Russians are protecting in that region. Putin isn't the bad guy here. And he is a hero to his people.

### **@sharkie1276**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=B_al2wgk49Y&lc=UgwoGSYn7WJ7gOjsMjt4AaABAg)

I am usually a fan of your lectures, but this one had a lot of misinformation that was presented as fact. I will just clear up a few things for people scrolling through the comments if they are interested.

1. Claim that prof made that US expected UA to win the war right away is false. This is a well known fact, backed up by the fact that the west was afraid to send weapons to UA in the beginning due to the concern that Russia would capture them all (due to winning). 
2. The claim that the prof makes about Russia winning the war is questionable at best. It depends on the definition of winning, but so far, every goal that Russia has set, has been abandoned. First it was to take over Kyiv in 3 days which failed, then it was to take over the eastern oblasts which they have yet to do in 2025 and are nowhere close. Fact is that Russia is failing to achieve every objective they set, which is hard to call a win. Ukraine is clearly losing this war over time as well, although it is dealing a lot of damage while doing so.
3. Prof brings up multiple points how Russia can expand the war if it wanted to, but chooses not to because its favourable for NATO to dump their equipment there. This is simply false. Russia does not expand the war, because it has no capacity to do so. 

Russia has a social contract with its citizens, which in simple terms is "government does whatever they want with foreighn policy, as long as it doesnt effect the average person". To expand the war, means they need to bring in the mobilized and reservists which is currently sitting in Russia, . By involving them, they create a great risk of civil uproar which Kremlin is very afraid of (think back to 2022 mobilization). This is why right now, the only troops fighting in Ukraine are volunteers for money. The reason why they needed North Korean help to fight in Ukraine, is because Russia has no more troops to pull from unless they want to risk a civil war with the government by forcibly mobilizing troops. 

On the topic of why Putin wont declare peace/end war, is because in simple terms, they cant financially. Ending/pausing the war means many troops returning home, which would soar the unemployment. There are many other problems that Russia will face when the war ends, which are actually bigger and much dangerous problems rather then the ones they face now while keeping the war going. Therefore, he cannot end the war and destroy the Russian economy further without something to show for it. And since Russia has failed all of its set objectives so far, Putin has to keep the war going until they get what they want, otherwise Russian people will think this was all for nothing and civil war will begin. 

These are just a few points I remember from watching the lecture. Please dont believe everything that comes out of a Prof's mouth, they can be wrong too. Lost some respect on this one for sure. Feel free to fact check everything I said.

Okay, interesting point of view, of course; there is some food for thought here. But the author fails in objectivity. It's full of not even propaganda, but self-imagination. While I agree with the US-Iran-Israel statement, how could you even possibly agree with simple logic like Russia using Ukraine as a 'black hole' for NATO? After this statement, it's enough to stop listening further. 

Who is wasting proportionally and nominally more resources in the war - Russia or NATO? Lets do the simple  fucking simple fact-checking: Russia wasted almost $200 bn in 2025 (goddamn 7% of the GDP) on this war, while overall support from allies is around $100 bn (it's 1% on average). That video was recorded in June 2024. You stated that the eastern region is occupied and the war is lost for Ukraine. Still, it's not fucking conquered after almost 2 years, what a fucking delulu. 

Okay, you proclaimed that Ukrainians don't want to fight and the average age of the soldier is around 42. The average age of Russians is 38. For the Ukrainians, the goal of the war is clear—it's their actual existence. Remind me, please, what were the 'clear goals' at the beginning of this 'special military operation'? Denazification? Demilitarization? 

You also stated that Russians are pretty okay with the war. Okay. But a war economy, to survive, needs conquests, and they are failing at it. China is buying gas and oil with big discounts, giving technology instead. The economy is literally stuck with high bank loan rates (around 15%). It's that high to protect from inflation (around 8%). While Ukraine suffers really much more, it has financial and military support from west. So, who is literally wasting resources? 

It's really dumb to say that it is 'healthy' for Russian society to wage war. Yet you did not see the root causes like bad economy, corruption, alcoholism. Actually, a few more words about alcoholism: Russian soldiers are so depressed on the battlefield and after it that, when they move back to civilian life, they usually can't comprehend its 'normality,' starting to drink more, raping, killing people, etc. If the USA is Satan, then Russia itself is a hell on earth. 

Last point - losses. Russian big losses are indisputable. They are severe and large, especially in 2022 and 2025. The number of 500k losses on the Ukrainian side appeared only (!) at the end of 2025, while Russia lost nearly 1.2 mln. Overall, we have Ukraine as Iran and Russia as the US, but you don't want to see this. So tell me, if Putin failed to get the eastern region in Ukraine, what kind of genius is he? Oh, maybe he is protecting Christianity? By having more Muslims and mosques in Moscow than in the whole EU? It's clear subjectivity; even knowing that your points might actually be true or at least interesting, it's hard to believe you due to this duality of thinking
